question referred under the 'blue card' procedure the disagreement between the three european institutions, the commission, the council and the parliament, has certainly led to the failure to define the new financial framework. you yourself have said that the internal market is suffering, research is suffering, agricultural policy is suffering. do you think it's fair to the citizens? citizens are increasing their trust in these institutions that cannot come to terms with a budget of more than six months. how can we transmit in our country the citizens' trust for projects in the european union when, behold, the heads of state and government are not able to make a decision?
blue-card answer. – i totally agree with your argument that of course we need to get the budget in order, and i think it is certainly not the fault of the parliament that at this moment in time we are not further ahead in these negotiations. i would like to make a further point on what you said. i think it’s also time that we got to work on a contingency plan because, if nothing is done, certain programmes will end at the end of this year, full stop. so a contingency plan on the part of the commission is greatly needed.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen, the discussions of these weeks and the ridiculous battles over decimals and zeroes reveal the double standards of those who say, in words, that they want an ambitious europe, but only in words. at this point, with the current proposal, we're not going to win any of the global challenges: i am not talking about the green deal, or the fight against climate change, or the digitisation of our economies, or, even less, the migration challenge, and i am thinking of greece. we are not going to do enough for research at a time when, for example, we need extraordinary funding to create a european scientific consortium that can find a vaccine for coronavirus. that is why we must act immediately by unlocking our own resources, starting with the common corporate tax base (cctb) and the web tax, and also by abolishing the shameful privilege of rebates and discounts which the richest countries in the union still enjoy and which they would not like to give up today. with britain leaving, there is no longer any justification: i say that i want an ambitious europe and that ambitious europe must have an ambitious budget, otherwise we are only fooling ourselves.
madam president, mr president michel, wherever you are at this moment, you are not respecting the european treaty. it is not the european council that has to negotiate the mff with the parliament, but the council of ministers. it's never happened before, it's the first time it's happened. we, the european parliament, should be negotiating the horizontal files. we have power there, co-decision power, and we should not, under any circumstances, abandon the proposal that parliament made in november, a very, very good proposal. i think we should also look at what an appeal to the european court of justice would mean, because it is obvious that those in the council are not respecting the treaty and i think that such actions should be met with similar actions.
madam president, i shall not speak today about figures or programmes - first because it has already been done here and also because that position is well known - but i would like to add four points which are also important for the negotiation. first, flexibility is a good thing. yes or yes. and i am very concerned to see that not only is the council not moving forward in flexibility, but that in every paper it has on the table there is less and less. we've seen it with the economic crisis, we've seen it with the refugee crisis. we need flexibility in the budget to adapt. second, the contingency plan. it is no longer an option, but a necessity, because we are clearly behind schedule and it will certainly not be the fault of this parliament, which has been waiting two years to negotiate. so, now, there's no rush, there's no emergency and certainly no pressure. let's take our time and make a good deal. and it is clear that, in addition, the circumstances we are living in now make flexibility and a contingency plan even more necessary, because we are going to have to keep adapting. thirdly, to remind the council and its president - even though it has had to go - that an agreement in the council is not the end of any process, but the beginning. and of course, if you go where your discussions are going, you will have a very difficult time negotiating with this parliament because we do not like that proposal at all and we would reject it. and finally, the trading box and all the contents inside. mr michel said earlier that we link the rule of law to the financial framework - that is very commendable - but they certainly do not have the power to decide how or who makes those decisions, and so on in many programmes. so i remind you that you have codecision with this parliament.
i have already pointed out on another occasion that you should not take charles michel seriously. yes, he was the ideal candidate for emmanuel macron: french-speaking and slavishly serving the fanatical federalists merkel and macron. but to think that he would therefore also be a good representative of the member states as head of the european council is a complete misconception. apart from the immoral nonsense of wanting to increase the budget of the european union instead of unanimously and with what the citizens want to moderate the agenda now that the united kingdom is going to spend its money on its own people first and no longer on the european union, charles michel is the last person to entrust you with a budget. at the head of the last belgian government, which was then supposed to put the so-called socio-economic and budgetary order in place and postpone the country's political reforms, michel left a veritable budgetary mess of between 12 and 15 billion euros on an annual basis: so the opposite of what was planned. you've been warned.
i have to say, madam president, i have listened carefully to mr michel. i would like to say to him: please agree on a figure for the european budget in the european council, but agree first on the kind of eu you really want, and then please finance it adequately! we've been in the same situation for years. on sunday, the speeches call for more innovation, more border protection, more encounters, more climate action, and now a common defence policy. on monday the member states are not prepared to provide the money. but on tuesday, they will say again how bad it is that the eu is not getting the problems solved. ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen, i think we need a budget to finance the common objectives. we need a budget that's adequately protected. we therefore need to be bound by the rule of law in the member states, and mr michel's last proposal was far from adequate. and we need a different situation in terms of revenue.
the commission has already made a number of proposals. i think we must be careful not to risk budget cuts which will jeopardize our great european objectives for the next few years. as chairman of the committee on agriculture, i am of course very keenly aware of the agricultural budget. let us look at the figures presented by president michel: we're going from 382 billion between 2014 and 2020 to just under 330 billion for 2021 to 2027. so it is cut by over 53 billion, and that is a 14% cut. ladies and gentlemen, this is unacceptable. how, then, are we to achieve the objectives of the european green deal? how are we going to implement the farm-to-fork strategy? how is biodiversity maintained in europe? we must do this with the farmers, not against them. that is why we need a budget that is able to cope with the situation so that agriculture can make its contribution.
madam president, i am one of those who believe in the european vision, who care and love the european union, and that is why i want a strong, proud, leading force in humanity, the european union. i want a european union which can strengthen its borders, create a single defence and security and a european army, so that it can defend itself from the sovereignty of the small island of cyprus to the sovereignty of all the countries of the european union. that is why i want a european union with a strong social face, with a digital single market, with policies that deal with green growth, with the fight against cancer, with a policy that creates working conditions for young children, a european union that responds to visions and aspirations. let's not kid ourselves: we must speak clearly to the european citizens. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. in conclusion, madam president, i would like to say that european citizens are concerned when their money is given to countries and those who do not apply the principles and values of the european union, and one country that receives this money, and europeans are complaining about it, is turkey, which should not receive a single euro from the european budget and the multiannual financial framework.
madam president, the minds of our leaders are naturally focused on the challenge of dealing with the virus, the coronavirus, but they have to make space for this issue about the future budget. i’ve been through several years where the debate on the budget has been tense and extremely difficult. i think this time it’s worse, and i suppose the uk departure is being used partially as an excuse to do less rather than more. all of us who deal with citizens in the member states know that people come to us looking for support, looking for money for many projects, and we have policies that need to be funded. the chair of the committee on agriculture and rural development, norbert lins, has already identified the issue for farmers, and there are serious threats to the agriculture budget from the various proposals on the table. this is not acceptable, because we want farmers to do more in the coming years rather than less, but if there is not sufficient funding it will be very difficult for them to do that. we need the leaders to act and to act now.
madam president, commissioner, public action, to be credible and effective, needs projects and political will, leadership and budgets. the problems we are trying to tackle in the union - climate change, the transformation of our production and energy model, the transformation of linear energy into circular energy, the immigration crises - require concerted action. and without a reasonable agreement on this multiannual financial framework we leave the european institutions without the tools. then it's very easy. when a problem is too much for us, it is much easier to blame everything on brussels. without a budget, without instruments, without strong european institutions, it is easier to endorse the european union through inaction, through our own mistakes, through our inability to reach agreements, through national misery and selfishness. this parliament, which is co-deciding - let us not forget - will not accept a bad agreement.
madam president, the united kingdom's withdrawal from the european union has created a sizeable budgetary gap which will not be easy to fill. migration challenges, the coronavirus pandemic and similar global challenges make this task even more difficult. when we adopt the multiannual financial framework, we must keep one thing in mind. it must uphold the european spirit of solidarity and the ambition of a balanced development of the union in the long term. otherwise, it will not fulfill its task and we will only create further divisions. there is a risk that less developed member states will be deprived of funds for cohesion and agriculture. it would be a step in the wrong direction and a clear message to current and future members that membership of the union no longer brings the value it once did to those lagging behind, and i believe that this would further weaken mutual trust and create the conditions for new divisions that would ultimately stabilise the union.
madam president, for me, the discussion around the mff really exposes what european values are, because the problem isn’t that agreement hasn’t been reached or that some programmes will be cut – the real problem is the areas of expenditure that are being prioritised by the commission and the council. this is a budget defined more than anything else by an enormous domination of expenditure on security and defence and on border management. the obscenity that the same arms companies that benefit from the wars that make people refugees are going to benefit from the contracts in us reinforcing our borders to keep them out. we’ve seen it in the decision last week to give eur 700 million to greece as a shield – a shield for europe and not a single word about the adherence to international law, the right to asylum, the need for a new migration pact. the pursuit of militarism in this eu will not meet the expectation of citizens and it’s a failure for all.
mr. chairman, colleagues, many people in this room today should be ashamed. colleagues on the left, on the left of me: years ago, you all justified and covered up for the turkish dictator erdogan. you pretended not to see that this is a dictator who uses human misery for his own political ends. you were the ones who said: "we'll manage that". well, no, you didn't. and you couldn't have done it. now i hear colleagues calling for the borders to be reopened, and i ask them: which of you, of those who have spoken out in support of illegal immigration, have you taken into your home at least one illegal immigrant from afghanistan, iran or eritrea? if there is, raise your hand and i'll admit i was wrong, but i know i wasn't. i know none of you would or have ever taken in any illegal immigrants, but you keep teaching us to think and reason. that, gentlemen, is hypocrisy and you should be ashamed of yourselves. (the president agreed to answer the question 'blue card' (rule 171 (8) of the rules of procedure))
blue-card question. – given that europeans have supported wars against peoples in these countries where the refugees have come from, and given that we – both militarily and economically – have absolutely destroyed their communities and their lives, do you not think that we should take some responsibility for these refugees, and do you not think that all european states should play their part? and yes, i’ve had an afghan refugee in my house for over a year and a half and i’ve had an iraqi kurd in it. (applause)
i cannot help feeling, mr president, and i think many citizens feel, that the european union has not really been present in these difficult times in the past. and i am not talking about competences, but about the coordinating and facilitating role that the union must be able to play. as for the member states, i would urge them to do their utmost, really their utmost, to maintain the socioeconomic fabric, to save jobs, to maintain purchasing power and also, and above all, to reduce the uncertainty which is already so great. the european union can and must provide support to these measures by not imposing administrative or budgetary obstacles and by allowing maximum flexibility in the field of these national initiatives. this is not the case, at least not with regard to the multiannual budget, which, as we all know, is on the back burner. the negotiations on this - which i think are now clear - will have to be reoriented to a very large extent. the multiannual budget and the negotiations will have to be maximum, flexible and agile in order to cope with this crisis and its consequences. i also assume that everyone now understands that we also need to have a contingency plan for this situation. finally, i would like to call for maximum focus on managing this crisis and its consequences. and if that means that other programmes, like the green deal, need to be redirected, so be it. the urgency, the priority and the ambition must now be elsewhere.
mr president, medical staff all across europe have been the heroes of this crisis, but a report published last month found that the european commission made at least 63 demands on member states to cut spending on health-care provision from 2011 to 2018 to meet the arbitrary debt and deficit targets of the stability and growth pact. these rules have hollowed out public services across europe, including health services. the president said that the stability and growth pact and state aid rules will be relaxed but they should be abolished for good. the commission proposes to use eur 37 billion, drawn from various funds, but these funds and projects will be needed more than ever when the crisis ends. the crisis will cost ireland at least eur 15 billion. will the citizens of ireland will be paying for years to come? you printed money for the banks, will you print it for the people? president von der leyen said the commission will mobilise all existing budget resources, so will you reallocate military spending to fight covid-19? the president says we must help each other. she said history is looking at us. well, history will damn our lack of solidarity. italy got more solidarity from china and cuba than it got from europe. it will damn us for failing to do enough to challenge us sanctions against countries like iran and venezuela. we have condemned them to die. capitalism has failed the people. we cannot go back to where we were.
the first thing to be remembered, mr president, is of course the victims and their families, and certainly all those who are working day in and day out in the health sector to combat this pandemic. this pandemic will also leave a deep economic trail. i think that compared to the financial crisis, this is worse, and it's going to get worse. it is therefore also logical that governments should do all they can to create the best possible conditions for economic recovery, while maintaining, of course - and this will be a delicate matter - the right balance between health care considerations and, on the other hand, economic recovery, jobs and prosperity. it is also the time to keep the big projects under review or to keep them under review. we remain committed to a sustainable transition with forward-looking innovation and competitiveness at its core. however, these objectives should be incorporated as far as possible into an economic recovery strategy. the eurogroup package will certainly provide support, but the big financial bazooka will of course remain the policy of the european central bank as it is now and as it has been drawn up. i call for solidarity, solidarity between member states and regions, whereby resources must go where they are needed most and be used in the right way.
mr president, of course the european union didn’t cause the virus, but the relentless pursuit of neoliberal capitalism has actually hampered our ability to deal with it and caused the damage – stripping our health services, leaving our workers exposed without sufficient protective gear, undermining and preventing the european centre for disease prevention from doing its work, and so on. all of these have caused the damage, so to respond to that crisis with a temporary suspension of some of those rules, but ultimately more of the same, is frankly not going to cut it. it is pathetic. it is the opposite of solidarity. an insufficient fund of eur 500 billion with strict conditions, where it is clear that ordinary people are going to be sitting with a bill later, is not going to wash. actually, it will be the end of the european union. we need radical change, we need debt neutralisation, we need zero—interest credit lines, we need the suspension of military expenditure, and so on. so can we please stop talking out of both sides of our mouth? if we’re really interested in solidarity, why haven’t we moved asylum seekers off the greek islands? why haven’t we dealt with orbán? why haven’t we spoken out against the sanctions damaging people? it’s not the chinese who are undermining the european union; it’s your own inaction.
mr president, i wanted to give a message to the president of the commission, ms von der leyen, but she’s no longer here. why? because i think that, rightly, she has spoken about the duties that our nurses, our medical people are fulfilling. but are we politicians fulfilling our duty? i’m not so sure about that because we have already been discussing this european master plan for many weeks in a row. my question is, what is the european commission waiting for? why can she not come forward with this trillion-sized european reconstruction and recovery plan? what is she waiting for? is she waiting for the european council? then we may be waiting eight years, as is the case of the banking union, because there is still no banking union after the financial crisis. the european council is playing ping pong. in february, the eurogroup could not agree on that plan. they sent it to the european council. then in march, the european council sent it back to the eurogroup. then the eurogroup, in april, a few days ago, was not in agreement with that part of the plan and sent it back to the european council. my message to the european commission is that this has to stop. you have the right of initiative. take your responsibility and come forward with what is asked by the whole plenary here, that is a trillion-sized european recovery and reconstruction plan, not based on mutualisation of that, not guaranteed by the member states – they have no money anymore, they will all be in deficit – but based on new own resources. it’s an historical moment for the european commission. we need a large type of initiative. you remember delors. there was a big crisis, an economic stagflation crisis, and he launched the single market and it lifted us out of the crisis. the same is needed now. the european commission has to act and not wait for the outcome of a council that will not decide next week. (applause)
we have seen, mr president, in the debate on the euro bonds, that the eurozone has become a zone of conflict. but i do not blame the italians, the spanish, the french, the germans or the dutch. i love all of them. i am actually blaming the eurozone, because the problem of the eurozone is the euro itself, mr verhofstadt. a single currency does not bring unity. on the contrary, it is an illusion, an illusion to have a monetary union with so many countries at the same time. that's never been seen in history. the differences are too great and the euro makes them even greater. and what is the consequence? fighting, everywhere, between countries, within countries, emotions, anger, blame, bitterness. mr verhofstadt, who is a passionate advocate of a european tax, an eu tax: the whole thing could fall apart if he wants it to. then we'll end up at mr. beck's steep exit. i feel that we are standing on the precipice, the north-south divide in the eurozone that can no longer be bridged. the eurozone is becoming a prison for everyone for other reasons. the portuguese prime minister has suggested that the netherlands should no longer participate in european decision-making and leave the eurozone. i thank the european prime minister for that suggestion, i'll think about it. what i see is debt on debt on debt. i see the tower of babel, ladies and gentlemen, and you all know how that ended.
mr president, covid-19 has certainly shone a light on obvious inequalities. what do we do about it? president von der leyen said ‘saying sorry only matters if we change our behaviour’. nurses, care-workers, bus drivers, supermarket workers, are now described as ‘key workers’. are we going to compensate them for the years of being under-valued, under-paid and now risking their lives? we can’t go back to normal, because normal wasn’t good, it was neo-liberalism. neo-liberalism lies at the heart of the eu and at the heart of everything that’s wrong with it. the pursuit of it is why we have not agreed to allow coronabonds for the likes of italy, ireland and others. it is why germany and the netherlands slammed the door in their face – they talk the talk on solidarity but they don’t walk the walk. the bank of england can issue their own currency and debt. can the eurozone? or would it not suit the more powerful countries? we want the eurozone project to survive but it has to change, it has to say goodbye to neo-liberalism. your resolution is calling to fight the virus by calling on russian and chinese propaganda to stop. that’s propaganda in itself! are there any adults in the room?
in writing. today's plenary debate will focus on the crisis caused by the coronavirus pandemic and the european response to it. in such a critical situation, when we debate and decide on issues of paramount importance, we should especially keep in mind the functioning of the law and the rule of law. we can't justify attacks by saying that our operations are unpredictable or inconsistent with the law. like all institutions, the institutions of the european union must operate according to predetermined rules and in accordance with the law. this is stated in article 2 of the basic treaty, which calls the rule of law the basis for the functioning of both the union and the member states. this requirement also applies to the european parliament. in my opinion, the current method of sitting and voting adopted by the bureau of the ep does not satisfy the requirements of legality, since it is contrary to the treaties and the rules of procedure. the commission has also concluded that the aid is compatible with the internal market within the meaning of article 107 (3) (c) of the treaty. the union particularly in today's situation of lack of legitimacy cannot afford to have anyone call into question the legal validity of its decisions. i hope that, taking into account my earlier proposals in this respect, the european parliament will take the necessary measures to restore lawful operation as soon as possible, in order to avoid any possible future legal disputes.
in writing. – the current epidemic situation has also proofed that future action is needed, regardless of the ‘sacredness’ of member state competences. it is intolerable that the health and quality of life of european citizens depends in particular on the given member state of the union they live in. national governments and the european commission have to strength their resolve and collaboration at eu level to combat the covid-19 outbreak. they have to adequately plan the recovery phases and better anticipate and manage future health emergencies. we need to develop a coherent set of guidelines and evidence-based strategies on the clinical management of covid-19 during the emergency and recovery phases. and we have to ensure a future european health response mechanism which will encompass an eu coordinated intensive care strategy that will oversee the timely and consistent supply of treatments, diagnostic tests, medical and technological equipment and at the same time involving doctors, experts and healthcare professionals and healthcare organisations in advisory panels and coordination groups at european level. we need the same standards, and we need the european minimum health service in every member state.
in writing. in its response to the coronavirus crisis, the european parliament, as part of the european peace project, must be the body that ensures that member states respect human rights and prevent authoritarian excesses during the crisis management. attacks on the judiciary and human rights are now commonplace. the european parliament must respond to these attacks and ensure that member states take seriously the hard-won european convention on human rights and take responsibility and ensure the rule of law. the situation in poland, hungary and greece is worrying. no government can use the coronavirus crisis to promote human rights and democracy. it is our task to ensure that europe acts in solidarity and provides protection to all people in europe during these difficult times. the incitement against minorities and the growing homophobia must end in a europe of solidarity. women must have the right to have safe and legal abortions. the refugees must be taken care of and greece must not be left alone on the refugee question again. the eu must stop the dismantling of democracy and the rule of law in hungary by victor orban. the corona crisis cannot be a vehicle for backlash.
in writing. the consequences of the coronavirus crisis can only be addressed at european level. this has become crystal clear after the wild border closures and export bans. it's just as clear that it's easier to lock down than it is to get out. the fundamental interdependence of our societies and economies means that one measure can have unintended effects on other areas of society. not only is a coordinated approach therefore necessary, but the nature of the measures will also be crucial. the joint resolution makes two things clear. firstly, most of the proposals are not new, but deal with familiar problems which were never taken up because of lack of political courage or national egoism: the commission has already taken a number of measures to improve the quality of the information it receives. it is time to put an end to old prejudices and move towards a europe which protects its citizens. secondly, there is also the debate on the job that there would be no money to implement the green deal. it is now time to use these resources wisely in the transition to a circular, sustainable and fair economy. the green deal is a strategy for sustainable growth. just what we need.
in writing. the eu has an important role to play in the fight against covid-19 and its consequences. the danish social democrats are therefore very pleased that they have been able to reach agreement on ambitious economic aid packages for the member states. and it is good when danish tripartite negotiations on wage compensation inspire the launch of sure. at the same time, we welcome the eu's leadership in coordinating the purchase of protective equipment and medical equipment, the building of pan-european stocks to prepare us for future health crises, and the creation of 'green corridors' at border crossings to ensure that european citizens do not experience shortages of goods and that our healthcare workers continue to receive vital equipment. although health policy is and should remain largely a national concern, eu cooperation can support member states' response to the pandemic and mitigate the long-term economic consequences through sound decisions. that is why we support the joint motion for a resolution. the resolution is not without problems, however. it is very worrying when the current crisis is used to push for deeper european integration. this is not the time. we also have reservations about the unequivocal support for increasing the overall framework of the erdf and the proposal for pan-european bonds.
in writing. mr president, the corona crisis has put a new emphasis on the importance of self-sufficiency and security of supply. at its most extreme, it is currently reflected in the global reliance on china's capacity to manufacture critical protective equipment and respirators. the crisis is forcing europe to reassess its own security of supply and global supply chains. there are a number of industrial sectors where europe is highly dependent on imported materials and components. global supply chains are now long and often not transparent to companies themselves. in europe, the crisis has forced many industrial companies to shut down due to a shortage of components, as mobility restrictions around the world have put long production and logistics chains on hold. it is not the case that we should be totally self-sufficient, but europe should be able to ensure the continuity of critical production even in exceptional circumstances. the coronavirus crisis is a great lesson in that. although the risks of energy and electronics dependence have been addressed in the eu in recent years, food production, the pharmaceutical industry and now the supply of critical protective equipment have not been adequately assessed from the point of view of security of supply. it is certain that global supply chains will change as a result of the corona crisis. the eu must do more to ensure that european production is able to function even in exceptional circumstances. this may also be contributing to the acceleration of the transition to a circular economy. the new circular economy action plan, which the commission presented in march, aims to move from linear production chains to the circulation of materials and raw materials, and would reduce dependence on critical imported raw materials. it would be economically and environmentally efficient.
in writing. the deadly pandemic we're experiencing has proven that we are all equally weak in the face of the enemy. the greek government has shown decisiveness and promptness in managing the crisis, a fact which has been recognised internationally. despite the ten-year debt crisis that has shaken my country and turkey's consistently aggressive policy on migration, greece has stood up bravely and acted effectively in the face of an international problem, showing the way to large and powerful countries. the health crisis has knocked on everyone's door and drained even the most robust economies. we welcome the economic measures taken by the union to support european countries, but we need even stronger solidarity in practice now than ever before. the time is right for decisive policies which will not divide the european north and south, but which will lead to a massive recovery and shared responsibility. the issuance of a european bond will provide the necessary support to deal with the pandemic and its consequences. in these difficult times, our thoughts are with those who are fighting on the front lines and fighting an unequal battle with the invisible enemy. we owe these people our deepest gratitude and express our heartfelt condolences to the families of the victims.
in writing. covid-19 has exposed the weakness of eu institutions, which have failed to take swift and coordinated action to protect the public health of citizens. he exposed how irresponsible it is to push for a liberal approach to healthcare management and how painful the consequences are of pharmaceutical companies and medical equipment manufacturers moving outside the eu. as a result, hospitals today lack life-saving respirators and medical equipment, as well as the necessary personal protective equipment. the community's helplessness is symbolized by the dramatic appeal of the italian people, who have been asking the eu for help for weeks. the commission's initial response to the pandemic was late and the proposed action was insufficient in the face of the scale of the challenges facing europe today, which is engulfed by the pandemic. the burden of the fight against the coronavirus has fallen on the shoulders of the member states. some are doing better, like poland, and some are doing worse. in the face of the rising number of cases and deaths, and the economic impact of the crisis, we need not words of solidarity, but concrete, urgent action. this is no time for political attacks. those who continue to attack poland and hungary even during the pandemic are acting wickedly. we're on the front lines of the fight, but it can't be a political fight. it is time to learn from this crisis so that the eu can take care of the health of its citizens in the future.
we are in the midst of one of the greatest crises, health, social and economic. this crisis has affected all eu countries and has required measures that none of us could have imagined until recently. countries and their governments acted quickly and according to current needs. i'm glad they kept each other informed and coordinated their moves. whether it was at european council meetings or bilaterally. they have been able to show a considerable amount of solidarity, sending each other protective equipment, medical supplies, helping each other to repatriate their citizens and taking in patients. i regret the resolution that has been tabled all the more. it does not reflect reality and is mired in the traditional euro-federalist dream of a more integrated and centralised eu. instead of looking at how the eu can actually help the member states and citizens, it's about more powers for the european commission that it doesn't need and a bigger budget that the member states have to pay anyway. and of course, they continue to cling to the policies of the new green deal, the costly restructuring of the economy and of society as a whole. brussels does not need to be the centre of the world and the eu always has to play the first fiddle at all costs. on the contrary, the strength of our cooperation lies precisely in the fact that member states are able to respond more quickly, flexibly and effectively to specific situations and problems. we need to work together and do some things together, but there's no reason to centralize and unify everything.
the joint resolution under debate in the european parliament demonstrates the broad consensus among the representatives of the peoples of the union for a strong, coordinated and united response to the pandemic, in health, economic, social and environmental terms. i would stress the measures for enhanced cooperation proposed in the field of health, in particular the strengthening of the powers of the european agencies and the creation of a european response mechanism for health emergencies. i would also stress the call for the strengthening of all the coordination mechanisms in crisis management. the creation of an additional fund for research and development in the field of innovative medicines and vaccines to deal with covid19 is also a very worthwhile proposal. the proposal that the funds made available by the financing lines approved by the eurogroup do not have negative conditionalities and also apply to the economic and social consequences of the pandemic is key to mitigating the economic and social impact. the joint bond issue to finance the recovery plan is decisive, as is the proposal to strengthen and adapt the multiannual financial framework to the new circumstances. finally, i would stress the importance of the european green deal and the digital transition as levers for recovery.

ladies and gentlemen, yesterday we celebrated international nurses' day and the world health organization has declared 2020 the year of the nurse and midwife. i would therefore like to begin this plenary session by expressing my appreciation and thanks to the women and men in white coats who have provided essential health services with dedication, courage and humanity in such difficult times. it is our common duty to enable them to do their jobs safely and efficiently. we have already made hospitals, human resources, machinery and medical equipment available from one country to another, thanks also to the work of the civil protection: the european union is a great example of solidarity and a great asset to all our countries. today we feel the need to strengthen the european union's powers in the field of health: it is a commitment we must make to our citizens. parliament called for a european health response mechanism to prepare for and respond in a common and coordinated way to any type of health crisis. the covid-19 pandemic has shown the need for more proactive public health policy and greater and better cooperation to build resilient and accessible healthcare systems, and, above all, the need for greater solidarity in europe. seventy years ago, on 9 may 1950, the solidarity of six european countries was driving towards a europe of peace and security. a europe which, as robert schuman said, 'will be born of concrete achievements which create de facto solidarity'. this vision was the only way to ensure lasting peace and improve people's lives. it was an ambitious vision, but also very pragmatic, based on the horrors of war and the sacrifices made to end it. seventy-five years of peace, a common project, with many future challenges to face. the work is only just beginning and with our women, men and young people able to develop solidarity, as they have shown in this period of grave emergency, our task will be made easier. we have a great responsibility before us: we must live up to the expectations of the european citizens and re-launch a great reflection on the future of europe, together with immediate responses to the problems we face. this crisis is teaching us many things, but most importantly, that only together can we start again.

mr president, i would like to oppose the urgency proceeding for relief measures for air services on behalf of the gue/ngl group. obviously, we were all very happy to agree to this procedure last time round regarding ghost flights, as they had an immediate impact. but now here we go again with the proposed relaxation of a whole number of important regulations in this industry without any scrutiny from the european parliament. we believe this is an incredibly dangerous procedure. in essence, we are allowing the important relaxation of regulations in the case of ground handling up to the year 2023 without any input from the committee on transport and tourism (tran) and without any discussion in this plenary. it’s not good enough. there’s nothing so urgent that it couldn’t wait another month. meanwhile, the industry that’s benefiting from these measures has announced thousands of job losses and is flagrantly refusing to honour passengers’ legal rights to a cash refund. why are we allowing concessions to this industry without demanding in return the protection of workers’ and passengers’ rights? our point is: if we accept this urgent procedure, in essence, we’re saying we’re happy to shed crocodile tears about these concerns while at the same time giving away the very leverage we have to force a change in behaviour. to do that, we need time to discuss it and, therefore, we’re opposing this procedure.
on behalf of the gue/ngl group. – mr president, this is not the first time we are debating the situation in hungary, but the thing is that the fidesz regime, they happen to go all the time a little bit further. when you think that now it will stop, they manage to trample on democracy and rule of law and fundamental rights just a little bit more. and i think they also deliberately do this to get other countries and other governments to follow suit. and that’s why we have to react in this house. and we have, this time, using the covid—19 crisis as an excuse, ruling by decree without a sunset clause, threatening journalists and whistle—blowers, passing laws and policies that have nothing to do with the public health crisis, such as rejecting the istanbul convention or attacking trans people’s rights. so, some say it’s not our business, let them dismantle democracy in hungary if that’s what they want to do. well, no, because we stand in solidarity, as was said here before, with all those hungarians that fight for democracy, for rights every day. they are not alone. and secondly, we make laws together here. and the authoritarian regime in hungary will make laws that all of us will have to respect. and that’s not acceptable. so standing up for law and democracy and fundamental rights in hungary is standing up for rule of law and democracy for all of us. luckily in this house, there are many of us that will not let hungary dismantle democracy. and it’s all about our business. so the question is then, what is the commission doing? what is the council doing? when will the epp expel and actually break up with fidesz? it’s a shame on you to have them there. the commission, the infringement procedures, we are waiting for them and the council, you are so weak in your statement, it is not enough. we need you to come up with a conditionality clause in the new mff, you have to be extremely strict on that. and no recovery funds to member states that don’t respect the rule of law and democracy. it is time for the commission and the council to get into the game. this house will stand up for democracy and human rights.
mr president, i’ve only been a member of this house for less than two years, but i have to say, ladies and gentlemen, that i have lost track on the countless times we discussed the democratic backslide in hungary and in poland. our respective positions, including mine, are therefore well known. so, i will spare you my usual alarm about the power grab the orbán government is operating in hungary. today, i would rather wish to address the commission and the council. where were you when democracy in hungary was put on lockdown? why is the commission, guardian of the treaties, so reluctant to act on defence of european values? i have to say that i expect more than blunt and critical statements by commissioner jourová. and why is the council so much afraid of its own shadow to act on member states that are persistently corrupting treaties and our eu values? those are the real questions that need to be addressed today. it is time for action. we have enough of those words.
mr president, covid-19 in hungary: introducing special economic zones to take over companies of strategic importance and transfer local taxes to fidesz controlled county councils; changing legislation so that further big parts of revenues of municipal governments are taken over by the central government; rejecting the istanbul convention and respect for trans people; amending the criminal code to punish so-called ‘misrepresented facts’ with up to five years. i could easily go on and would only refer to developments over the last two months. everything to combat the virus looks more like combating opposition parties, municipalities, journalists, civil society. and how can the epp, calling itself the ‘people’s party’, still continue with flimsy debates about a potential expulsion of fidesz, leading to nothing? and how can you, commissioner jourová, say we still see no reason for infringement procedures? looking back over the past 10 years, what still needs to happen? and the council and the member states, we all know the article 7 procedure is completely deadlocked. will you now finally protect democracy and fundamental rights also for hungarian citizens, and are you ready to suspend eu payments to orbán? it’s high time to act.
mr president, listen: this european parliament defends the core freedoms of every citizen of the union, including, of course, hungary. the extraordinary powers of a government must also be accompanied by extraordinary scrutiny of what it does, extraordinary freedom of expression and the capacity for effective political criticism and the rules of the game that allow it. we represent 450 million citizens. we do not feel vassals of any government; we feel free and equal. and president orbán needs to hear it and all presidents need to hear it. under the state of emergency, under the pandemic, democracy is not suspended. and there will certainly be no loophole in the arbitrariness of the public authorities which the committee on civil liberties of this parliament does not look at, criticise and enumerate, and on which we do not act in this parliament. i must tell you that there can be no impunity and listen: we are not going to stop in this parliament.

mr president, there’s no doubt about it. democracy has been attacked in poland. democracy has been attacked in france. there is vicious racism, similar to hungary, in other states, but hungary has taken it a step further. there has been a blatant and clear-cut authoritarian takeover, and it’s not an accident. it’s the product of a 10-year assault on judicial independence, on media freedom, on civil society and the right to organise. a 10-year assault in which the council and the commission did nothing. we’ve had more of the same here today. there needs to be an immediate getting behind article 7 infringements proceedings. there needs to be an immediate mechanism to cease funding those who would trample on democracy. there needs to be a political ostracisation of those who undertake the types of actions that everybody knows are being engaged in in hungary today. because if we don’t do that, then all of the other states who have the same tendencies will go the same way. and we are betraying the citizens of hungary and all of those across the union.
madam president, we’re talking about addressing symptoms and not causes. the way we produce meat today, with thousands of genetically identical chickens and pigs stuck in massive factories – natural selection, an ecosystem service that provides real-time disease protection, is more, now, effectively a thing of the past. if we do not address industrialised livestock production, we are sure to have bigger and more lethal epidemics than this one. and we cannot win the fight against covid-19 and future infectious diseases unless we properly fund a public sector that values public health over profit. a health service should not be a business opportunity for big pharma in a health crisis. the chief executive of the french pharmaceutical giant sanofi said yesterday that they can deliver a vaccine to the americans, who want to get it first, because they get more funding. this is a company that’s subsidised by the french people. this is a company that distributed eur 4 billion to its shareholders during the pandemic. the covid-19 global response framework was announced on 4 may. what conditions will be attached to the fund? at the very least, public investment in a covid-19 vaccine and the therapeutic development should come with hard conditionalities attached, including real equitable public access, non-exclusive licensing, and an affordable pricing model. but even attaching these conditionalities would not deal with the root of the problem. we need to de-commodify vaccines and therapeutics. we should nationalise pharmaceutical companies, and we should do it now.
in writing. i am convinced that the recovery fund is a necessary instrument to overcome the crisis. this will give the mff the necessary added momentum. it is essential that the use of money borrowed from the markets is strictly monitored and that the european parliament is involved in the monitoring. i believe that parliament should be closely involved in the whole budgetary process, because it is the only european body with a mandate from the people. the money for economic recovery must be well-targeted and generate high added value. it would be unacceptable for all our money to be used to plug holes caused by poor and irresponsible policy decisions. conservative monetary policy must be respected and we must take the extreme seriousness of pushing debts onto our children and grandchildren. this is certainly not in line with the principles of responsible politics. the european commission has not yet clearly indicated from which sources it will start servicing the loans it will take out from the markets. certainly, the decision-making bodies of the member states of the european union and we as members of the european parliament have the right and the duty to know all the necessary details before making important decisions. i am convinced that the same emphasis on digital transformation is needed to boost the economy and increase european competitiveness. it is one of the most important factors affecting europe's competitiveness today and in the future.
in writing. europe is facing, for the first time since the second world war, an unprecedented crisis affecting all member states. european citizens are not satisfied with the measures taken so far at european level and expect the eu to give them more powers, more solidarity and a stronger response to the crisis. the new recovery fund is an ambitious union support project with increased resources, mainly through grants. however, significant support and immediate cash disbursement should be provided for the tourism sector, which has been hit hardest by the pandemic. tourism, which contributes around 11% to european gdp and accounts for 12% of employment in the eu, is losing around €1 billion in revenues monthly due to the pandemic. the situation is particularly difficult in many eu countries which are major tourist destinations, such as greece, where the tourism sector indirectly contributes over 20% to the greek economy. the greek government has already taken important measures to support the sector, but the response should not be from individual member states but from the eu as a whole. let us not disappoint our citizens!
in writing. the proposal for a european union recovery package presented by the president of the european commission, ursula von der leyen, is a disruptive development in the typology of the union's responses to systemic crises. it is a strong and supportive proposal. the scale of the crisis, the perception among citizens that only a coordinated response can deal with it and the adoption by the european parliament, by a large majority, of resolutions endorsing the developments by the direct representatives of the peoples of europe are all factors in this. investing in a green, digital and resilient europe, by adding the multiannual financial framework, own resources and the creation of a €750 billion recovery fund, using common debt issues and allocating €500 billion (66% of this fund) through grants, is a potentially robust response, which the council should adopt as soon as possible, so that the european parliament's negotiation and consent procedures and specific regulation allow the resources to reach where they are needed as quickly as possible.

on behalf of the gue/ngl group. – mr president, yes, i would like to support the request to postpone the vote on the ratification of the euro-mediterranean aviation agreement with the state of israel. this ratification has no actual practical implications as the agreement has been provisionally applied since 2013, but to adopt it now in the context of the announcement by the new israeli government of their imminent plans to annex one third of the occupied palestinian west bank, in flagrant breach of international law, is of substantial symbolic and political importance. to ratify this agreement now would send an inconsistent message from the european parliament. it would be perceived as an upgrade in bilateral relations with the state of israel, a positive for them and a complete shift in the eu’s long-standing policy of linking any upgrade in the relationship with israel to progress on conflict resolution. clearly this latest move from israel is only going to accelerate the conflict and the hardship on the palestinian people. we should not normalise israel’s violation of international law by ratifying this agreement now, and i’m asking colleagues to please support the postponement of this decision.
on behalf of the ppe group. – mr president, if one is not ready to speak for freedom everywhere, we cannot expect freedom to be granted anywhere. china continues to subvert hong kong’s autonomy, rule of law and fundamental freedoms, as well as international agreements. the latest unilateral decision by the national people’s congress of china to enact a national security law for hong kong seriously threatens the city’s autonomy, rule of law and fundamental freedoms. it is expected that the law will introduce a range of vague and draconian charges. two weeks ago, over 900 political leaders worldwide, including many of you, made a joint statement condemning this move. the joint motion for a resolution of this parliament provides a clear and principled stance on the situation in hong kong and upholds the values on which this union has been built, and which should and must direct our foreign policy, as stipulated in article 21 of the treaty. i therefore call on all members to support this resolution and to stand with the people of hong kong.

for the group gue/ngl. mr president, the european union's civil protection mechanism, in its original form, was set up to help member states deal with natural disasters. resceu capacity, a pan-european reserve of firefighting aircraft and helicopters, specialised medical equipment and other resources have been created. the pandemic has come and i am very pleased that resceu's capacity has also included medical supplies, such as personal protective equipment or laboratory equipment. the european union civil protection mechanism has become one of the few instruments of solidarity during the pandemic. but now we can make this mechanism much more efficient and faster. the experience of fires and pandemics has shown that the european union and the member states are not sufficiently prepared to respond to large-scale emergencies, which is unacceptable. where cooperation makes sense, it should be encouraged. and when is it more necessary than when life is at stake? i'm glad we could work something out.
member of the commission. the commission is not i'll speak german for a change. i thank you for your overwhelming support and your united view. but then mrs beigneux's last statement makes me want to clarify a few things. what we are discussing now is a necessary development of the existing mechanism, which, as some have quite rightly said, was originally designed to respond to natural disasters, and natural disasters do not occur - thank god - all at the same time in europe, so that this subsidiarity mechanism has worked. i have seen this time and again in my various roles as commissioner over the last ten years. we are now faced with a disaster which affects virtually all member states, and so their ability to help others is limited. we have seen, on a modest scale, how the situation has simply improved in some countries, for example, by transferring patients who needed intensive care to other countries where various beds were still available. but all of this was very limited, and we also had known problems with supplying a certain number of devices. all of this is to be addressed and eliminated by this new mechanism. there is no question of removing any competence from the member states. on the contrary, it is simply that we can prepare ourselves better for future crises by learning from the current situation, and that is all that is needed. i would also like to join in the thanks to my colleague mr lenarčič for his very intensive and dedicated work over the past months.

the agenda includes the report by mr jutta paulus, on behalf of the committee on the environment, public health and food safety, on the proposal for a regulation of the european parliament and of the council amending regulation (eu) 2015/757 to take due account of the global fuel consumption data collection system for ships (com (((2019) 0038 - c8-0043/2019 - 2019/0017 ((cod)) (a9-0144/2020).
member of the commission. – mr president, addressing shipping emissions remains a priority for the commission. the eu system to monitor, report and verify these emissions is an important step to integrating emissions into eu climate policy; but it is just a first step, because we aim to come up with plans to reduce overall emissions in the maritime sector. the key goal of our reporting system is to collect robust and verified co2 emissions data, to make those data publicly available, to stimulate the uptake of energy-efficient technologies and behaviours, and to inform our policymaking. since the entry into force of the data collection system of the international maritime organization (imo) in 2019, all large ships calling at ports in the european economic area have to report their full consumption and other information under two systems: the european and the international one. in accordance with the monitoring, reporting and verification (mrv) regulation, the european commission has now proposed to align, where appropriate, the eu mrv system with the imo data collection system. the aim is to preserve the objectives of the current mrv regulation by keeping its governance and ambition, while including technical adjustments to align the two regimes, simplifying compliance. the commission proposal does not affect the main thrust of the european system, which is to maintain the robustness and transparency of our rules. the report of the environment committee before you has received the commission’s full attention. as you know, some proposed amendments go significantly beyond the commission’s proposal to align the european global data collection systems, namely the amendment to extend the european emissions trading system (ets) to maritime transport and the amendment for large shipping companies to reduce their annual average co2 emissions by at least 40% by 2030. while the european commission fully agrees with the european parliament that the shipping sector needs to duly contribute to the implementation of the paris agreement and must achieve the union-wide climate neutrality objective, we don’t consider the review of the eu reporting rules to be the appropriate vehicle to include shipping in the ets or to introduce operational component density requirements. there is also no impact assessment for these two elements. there is a difference of opinion on the instrument, but our political goals are shared. as you know, the commission has already announced, as part of the european green deal, its intention to extend the ets to maritime emissions, and the impact assessment is being prepared. work on this is in full swing and the commission will come forward with the relevant legislative proposals to reduce the climate impact of shipping activities. let me stress, the commission will duly take into account the elements proposed by this parliament when developing its initiatives under the european green deal. for these reasons – and as we are still awaiting the council’s position on these elements – the commission has to reserve its position on the amendments concerning the extension of the emissions trading system and the carbon density requirements for ships. of course, the same applies to other amendments that were not in the original commission proposal. the commission looks forward to continuing discussions on other important aspects of its proposal. these include possible improvements to the scope of the regulation in terms of ship size, rules when there is a change of company, the reporting of cargo carried, and the review clause within the remit of the reporting system. let me assure you that the commission remains committed to reducing emissions in the maritime sector. let me also assure you that the upcoming 2030 climate target plan will show not only this commitment but also the ambition to get it done. we will work hand in hand with this parliament on our pathway to climate neutrality in shipping and beyond.
on behalf of the s&d group. – mr president, europe is going through this huge transition from our history as a coal and steel union to the climate neutrality union. everyone needs to contribute in that transition and today the shipping sector is not part of it. they sail away and do their own thing. it’s not acceptable. we need to make sure that everyone is part of this transition if we are to deliver what it is our right to do, as politicians. we know that the shipping sector also has a very negative impact on the climate and it risks to have even more so in the future. it’s extremely worrying that it, in fact, has proceeded to raise its contribution to emissions between 50 or 250% to 2050, when we are supposed to be climate neutral. we in the group of the progressive alliance of socialists and democrats (s&d) refuse to stand passive and therefore we are very happy that in this monitoring, reporting and verification (mrv) system is a good piece of legislation for reporting emissions, but now we finally have an emissions trading system (ets) proposed on the table and we believe this will be very important to take this sector under its, rightly, demand to contribute. we also demand that every ship will reduce its emissions by at least 40% by the year 2030. it should not be free to destroy the future – not the climate, not the environment – and we know that the environment is also hit by this sector today; biodiversity, air quality, we need to do something about that. therefore, we would like to have a fund for the sea to help the sector improve on all this and we would also like a big part of this fund to go towards helping the sector to transition. so this is a really clever proposal that we worked on together. i would like to say to the rapporteur and to all the shadow rapporteurs that i have been very honoured to work with you. i really think we have something good on the table.
on behalf of the gue/ngl group. – madam president, the committee on the environment, public health and food safety (envi) showed real ambition in voting for a radical expansion of the scope of the commission’s proposal. we are proposing to include shipping in the eu emissions trading system (ets), without further delay with this regulation. market-based solutions for the climate crisis, such as ets, have generally failed. however, in this instance we are proposing to frame shipping’s inclusion in the ets with a binding regulatory target of 40% emission reductions per transport work by 2030. the rapporteur’s report is mainly concerned with emissions efficiency. shipping emissions are directly related to global trade and consumption. therefore, in order to reduce absolute greenhouse gas emissions from maritime transport, in addition to emissions per transport work, the eu must promote and pursue a global de-growth strategy rather than the new growth strategy proposed in the european green deal. in amendment 71, i also propose to delete the exemption for warships. militaries are massive polluters. the us military, for example, doesn’t just destroy the lives of more people and communities than anyone else, as julian assange has highlighted, they’re also one of the biggest polluters on the planet, and i see no good reason to exempt any warships.

in writing. the annual assessment of the president of the european commission was first held at a time when the eu was also in serious crisis. since then, commissions have come and gone, national governments have changed, but one thing has remained almost constant: the crisis. this is not necessarily a bad thing, because crises are a stimulus for continuous improvement, and our european community can only get better. as a hungarian representative, i could speak in this debate about the community-destroying policies of anti-eu, illiberal governments and condemn the leaders of the union for having for years stood by helplessly as they were made fools of by political adventurers who trampled the rule of law to dust. as a hungarian representative and one of the drafters of the concept of the european health union, i would like to emphasise the importance of healthcare in the debate on the future of the union. health, which has been a third-rate area of competence for the eu as a member state. but the coronavirus has shown that without strong health systems that can provide quality care, there is no health, but there is no economy that can operate predictably. this is a turning point, a time of radical transformation: imbalances between the health systems of the member states cannot be sustained any longer. a solution is needed that leaves the member states' competences, which many people fear too much, but guarantees quality care and patient safety everywhere. that is the concept of the european health union. i think it's a big breakthrough that the chairman of the committee is behind this case.
in writing. the past year has been a challenging one for europe as a whole, and the autumn ahead looks no easier. in the future of europe, we have four major challenges to tackle this autumn. the key is to boost sustainable economic growth and competitiveness. after the acute corona crisis, europe faces a major economic challenge. as ursula von der leyen pointed out, to boost growth, it is important to remove corporate bureaucracy and barriers to the internal market, strengthen capital markets and invest in the green deal and digitalisation. at the same time, the most important foundation of the european union - the common values - must be safeguarded. democracy, freedom and the rule of law must be guaranteed in all eu member states. we must also be able to find a common line on immigration policy. we can't afford to fail again. the fourth essential theme is the common foreign and security policy. the union is too slow and too rigid in this respect, as has been shown by recent events in russia and belarus. in international crises, we need to be able to react more quickly. the foreign policy decision-making process must therefore be modified and made more effective by increasing the use of qualified majority voting. this year was supposed to be a big year for climate policy in the european union. despite the time and energy consumed by the corona crisis, the european union must remain committed to being climate neutral by 2050. i support the commission's proposal to tighten the 2030 emission reduction target to at least 55% to ensure this.
in writing. the best term to describe the state of the european union is 'stillness'. our continent has been overwhelmed by one of the greatest crises in history, and yet brussels continues to pursue its policy, displaying a total lack of self-criticism. opposition forces are accused of extremism, and consequently ignored and excluded from any decision. we hear daily calls for the rule of law and democracy, but when a political leader and former minister is put on trial for defending his country, italy, no one moves a finger. on the geopolitical front, the eu is showing incoherence and inertia towards china and turkey, certain that it can count on the weaknesses of a system based on the special interests of some member states: the commission has not yet taken a decision on the matter. the eu should reflect carefully on what has happened in recent months, remembering that member states will only be able to really get back on track after suspending some of the rules imposed by brussels. there's no more time to waste. if there is no real change, europe will continue to drift towards a decadent path which will be its end.

rapporteur. – madam president, today, president von der leyen called for european buildings to become less wasteful, less expensive and more sustainable. as rapporteur for this report, maximising the energy efficiency of the european building stock, i know we can do this. i want to thank the shadow rapporteurs for their close cooperation and support during negotiations. we now have a strong and united position on our demands for the upcoming renovation wave strategy. this is a good news story – we can create jobs, tackle energy poverty, improve air quality and reduce emissions in these extraordinary times. it tackles the health crisis, the economic crisis and the climate crisis. yesterday, weather records were broken here in belgium. 35° of heat is not normal and we know that 40% of emissions that cause this come from buildings. with the right investment, buildings can reduce emissions rather than raise them. the report says we must fight energy poverty by working with neighbourhoods and communities to ensure healthy and safe living conditions and ensure affordability. we must raise national targets and revise the energy efficiency directives and the energy performance of buildings directives and binding minimum energy performance standards are required. we also require integrated renovation programmes, a holistic approach from rooftop solar and e-mobility, from heat pumps to district heating is required. so let’s use recovery funds for the renovation way. i endorse the president’s call for a new bauhaus. as an architect, i welcome this. so let’s harness the creativity, the design skills and the demand for change and let’s start with the worst performing buildings and the communities most at risk. let’s lift people out of energy poverty and give them the buildings and the homes that they deserve.

madam president, setting a 55% net target for 2030 is climate change denial. the intergovernmental panel on climate change (ipcc) has said that in the gap between 1.5 and 2 degrees, hundreds of millions of lives are at stake. a target of 55% would mean the eu has given up on any chance of limiting global temperature rises to 1.5 degrees. to avoid climate breakdown, we have to cut world emissions at least in half by 2030, and get to zero by 2050. but this is a global average target. given their greater responsibility for historic emissions, the better—off nations – like in europe – must do it a lot faster. setting a climate neutrality target for 2050 is also a climate change denial. climate neutrality is not a decarbonising strategy. why are we continuing to prop up the fossil fuel industry? we need real zero carbon emissions by 2040 and we cannot rely on negative emissions technologies that might never even exist. scientists say that we will fail if we keep growing the economy at the same rate. gdp may speak for the welfare of capitalism but not for the welfare of humanity.

what did we commit to? what have we given up? what have we bleached? what have we legitimized? absolutely not at all. mr lopez, i understand that if i were in your shoes and had a child in the spanish embassy, i would have an emotional burden that would surely make me say the same thing you have said, but please do not say that it is a secret mission. secret missions are not communicated by letter to all member states. don't say we went to whitewash mr. maduro's regime. you know my opinion of that regime. you know that your son was received at the spanish embassy when i was foreign minister and that i am very proud to have been able to contribute something to that. my commitment to democracy in venezuela does not have to be validated by you; it is proven by the facts, and what we have done now does not deny it. it's a risky endeavor, you know. it's a questionable effort. you are criticizing it much more than it deserves, and on the other hand also, to try to find a solution and help the opposition to feel stronger and supported by the international community and the european union when talking to the government. unfortunately, we have not succeeded, if the government of venezuela, the regime of mr. maduro, had told me, before i sent the mission, "it is not worth coming because i do not plan to change the election date, because the constitution forbids me", as they are saying now, then yes, certainly, we would not have gone. what's the point? but that wasn't so clear, that wasn't so clear, in all the discussions that we've had directly, through the opposition, that wasn't so clear. there was a small glimmer of hope. there was a small possibility, and i felt compelled to explore it, to try to see if there was something that could be done to push this political dialogue that, in the end, will one day be the only solution for the people of venezuela. and, believe me, your contribution to this case has been pitiful.
