vice-president of the commission. – mr president, honourable members, minister, thank you all for your interventions. let me start with the three areas where i think we all agree before addressing some of the specific points raised. first, we all agree that the arrival of the covid—19 vaccines does not mean that the pandemic is over. i think this is a strong message that should come out of this debate. we also agree on the importance of the three golden principles that we set out some time ago, namely equal access, affordability and safety of vaccines. and finally, we do agree on something that is particularly important these days, that in europe we do not improvise on safety. nor do we cut corners on the procedures that we have established, and the european model is that independent regulatory authorities have to do their job and politicians have to base their authorisation on sound, scientific evidence. i think the strong support that all of you have shown through this process says a lot. let me now address three specific issues where some of you have raised the need for further explanations from the commission. let me start first with the issue of confidentiality. we fully recognise the importance of transparency in this process. transparency is a guiding principle in all our work in the commission. it is publicly known with which companies we have been negotiating, what type of vaccines and the process through which we have been doing this together with our member states, but it should be clear that we are indeed bound by the confidentiality clauses in these contracts. disclosure of any confidential information at this moment in time could undermine the ongoing negotiations we have with other companies, and, as the european union, we would need to keep as strong a negotiating position as possible. we cannot unilaterally decide to disclose confidential information such as information on prices under these contracts unless the companies themselves agree to it, and we are in the middle of negotiations which are still ongoing. however, on many occasions commissioner kyriakides has confirmed to you in this house that the commission is ready within the contractual constraints and according to the rules for transmission of confidential information to the european parliament to make information from these contracts available to nominated members of this parliament with specific arrangements once the sensitive ongoing negotiations are concluded. another issue that has been raised by many of you is the issue of trips and intellectual property protections. i want to repeat in this house that we will ensure that intellectual property rights are not an obstacle to the universal access to vaccines, including in developing countries that do not have the means to cope. mr lange asked for a ‘yes’ or ‘no’ answer, but i think my answer is pretty clear – that there is an ongoing discussion now in the wto on the idea of a waiver of intellectual property rights and protections and there are also important flexibilities so that the relevant trade agreement – trips – could allow us to reach the same objective. but, in any case, i want to assure everybody here that we are ready to continue this discussion so that nothing stands in the way and that more vaccines get to where they’re needed worldwide. finally, some of you have raised the issue of campaigns. of course, we are very sensitive and we shall encourage our member states to continue with their national plans and public information campaigns on vaccination. we will have our own european campaign, if you like, which will be unveiled in the days to come. we do not compete with our member states on information, but i think we can provide a useful complement to this process. and to that extent we will not only explain factually the benefits of the vaccines that are put on the market, but we would also like to mobilise important role models, important known europeans. some uefa players have already kindly accepted our invitation to participate in our european campaigns, so stay tuned for that. the rollout of the vaccines is a challenge of historic dimensions. this is a historic moment, and after all the work we’ve been doing for months, i am convinced that we’ll get it right.
the president of the council. the commission has already made a number of proposals. the debate here today in the european parliament has shown the range of all the arguments: one is not doing well enough, the others are already warning about the possible consequences even though the vaccine has not yet been approved. yes, ladies and gentlemen, we are living in a risky situation, and if every headache medicine has side effects, it is not impossible that this vaccine will have side effects. no one's saying it's side effect-free, either. we've invested a lot of money to shorten the typically long years it takes to get a vaccine approved, to research it, we've spent many billions to get people the protection they desperately need. no one in the council, no one in the commission, no one in his right mind is going to stand here and say that there is any solution that promises a 100% guarantee. i am rather astonished that some members - especially in this section - are standing here and arrogantly talking about the different strategies of the member states, although nationalists and populists in particular have repeatedly given the impression that this is a flu, a cold that passes away immediately, that we do not have to take this seriously, that wearing a mask is superfluous. all these demonstrations, where people's lives were put at risk, where police officers were attacked, there is also a political responsibility for this. i want to make that very clear. i can't just stand here and say, "yes, i have a 100% solution here". what we are doing now remains fraught with risk. my only request to you, mr president, is that we, as political leaders in national governments, in the commission and in parliament, should at least be able to have a fact-based, reason-based and fearless debate. outside of this room, there's enough fear already -- we're experiencing it in our own families. so we have a responsibility to provide security, as far as that goes in a democracy. of course, there are others who are faster. the russians and the chinese, they're vaccinating everywhere. yes, but these are vaccines, they're not even approved yet. i have seen it myself: a member of parliament has just mentioned it, and some media, but also some political forces, are playing with it. we're just taking our time. we have to make sure that even though the research on this vaccine has gone very, very fast, really revolutionary fast, that in the end the risks, including the side effects, are manageable, accountable. some members have made it clear, and for very, very good reasons, that it is not enough for us in the european union to look after ourselves alone. there must always be a strategy for how we can make a global contribution. but i have the impression that the european union - and there is still much to be done - has already done a lot. the follow-up to the covax facility involves concrete financial contributions, some eur 860 million having already been paid by the eu and the member states. of course, it will also be about creating the conditions so that, once again, access to this vaccine is not measured by how strong a country is economically, how much money its citizens have or where in the world they live. we must set a good example as the european union. i think i understood that message today, and i want to assure you once again that we as a council are also trying to live up to this responsibility. we have a long, long way to go, ladies and gentlemen. if the first vaccine is finally available for use, it is not a miracle weapon. but it's a very, very important signal to take people's worries away. because the economic and social consequences are, of course, devastating. but in the end, it's every person's safety that counts, and every day counts. every day that the vaccine is approved earlier can help to reduce the number of people who die. i am pleased to have heard this signal of health as a priority as a message from almost all speakers, so thank you very much. this debate has made an important contribution to highlighting the european union's responsibility in this serious health crisis. but we're not just talking, we're acting. this has been expressed in a very impressive way in the past weeks and months.
the debate is closed. the commission shall be assisted by the committee of the regions.
in writing. the importance, the importance and the power of the european union can be seen in these very days. without the joint action of the united eu states, the coronavirus vaccines would not be available for the germans or the french for a long time. especially for the slovaks. thanks to the eu, our citizens will be among the first to be protected, at the same time as germans, luxembourgers, swedes or czechs. since i am not indifferent to people with weakened immunity and many other diseases, i will definitely get vaccinated.
in writing. mr president, the corona pandemic has tested the european union's ability to act and its basic principles. the crisis has a lot to teach us for the future. however, the commission has managed to act relatively well in the corona crisis in those areas where it has the power of decision and the scope for action. the most obvious example of this is the vaccines under consideration. the european union has made it possible to invest heavily in vaccine research and to conclude joint vaccine procurement agreements in a short time. neither of these could have been achieved by any member state alone, especially on such a timetable. it would be desirable in future to see a greater appreciation not only of european cooperation but also of science and research. at the same time, i must say that i am disappointed by the coordination between member states, particularly in the area of travel restrictions. the coronavirus will be with us for a long time to come and it is vital that member states are able to act more coherently in the future to contain the pandemic. cooperation between member states is key to ensuring the most efficient and equitable distribution of vaccines and to responding to vaccine criticism. the commission's new openness to the health union and the larger eu4health programme will help to strengthen cooperation in tackling health threats. the corona is a global crisis, and we need not only eu action but global cooperation to respond to it. the european union must take responsibility for ensuring that vaccines are distributed even in the poorest countries. the commission is rightly committed to supporting the development of vaccines, medicines and diagnostics and equitable global access to vaccines. the covax vaccine mechanism, which promotes equal access to vaccines, has an important role to play and now needs the support of both the eu and the member states to make it possible.
the agenda includes the discussion of the statements by the council and the commission on the eu strategy for a security union ((2020/2791 ((rsp)). i would remind members that there is no catch-the-eye procedure for all the debates of this part-session, nor will there be any blue-card questions. in addition, remote assistance is provided by the european parliament liaison offices in the member states.
the president of the council. the commission is not our joint work to improve the security of our citizens and our societies is a constant test, and with the council conclusions on internal security and a european police partnership we have launched a necessary process of reflection to significantly improve the future of internal security, especially with regard to the european dimension. and we 've also accomplished a few things: the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of the health of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos is a very good one. we have identified a number of priority areas, including instruments that have already been agreed but which need to be implemented more quickly and better, and of course also instruments where there are gaps and which need to be better addressed in the future. the recent terrorist attacks, ladies and gentlemen, have made it alarmingly clear that the online environment plays a very central role in providing a platform and a voice to violent extremism and terrorism. several ongoing and future initiatives are making a significant contribution to combating this development, such as the proposal on terrorist content online, which has just been the subject of a political agreement thanks to excellent cooperation between the european parliament and the council, and the recent proposal for a law on digital services. the attacks also highlighted the challenges facing law enforcement, judicial and security authorities in accessing digital information, especially when it is encrypted. we need to ensure that our law enforcement and judicial authorities have the same legal powers and competences online as offline. it is in the interest of all of us, honourable members, that the internet does not become a lawless space or a safe haven for criminals, and there is actually a very, very simple rule, honourable members, that we simply have to implement: what is illegal offline is and must be illegal online. of course, we must find a balance here, and we do not have a patented recipe for that either. this is also subject to democratic debate, especially with civil society and experts. this is the balance between security on the one hand and the fundamental rights to privacy on the other. and to do that we need a broad, inclusive and transparent dialogue between the various responsible bodies, and of course the european union has a very important role to play in this, given the crossborder nature of crime. and so the use of artificial intelligence, access to data, the use of big data in law enforcement and internal security are all relevant issues that we need to address very quickly. i would also like to emphasize once again our agencies, which play a very important role in this, in a forward-looking and responsible security agency which also supports the member states in their tasks. we need to see how we can ensure that all law enforcement officers have access to all the information they need, how we can make more of the wealth of information already available in our information systems, how we can work together more concretely and exchange information more efficiently across borders every day. now, one or two of you might say, "yeah, why do you talk like that? i wish we had done it a long time ago. i know! it is also, of course, largely because internal security is one of the core competences of the member states. and there are no automata. and of course, we have to be more proactive here. and we must also learn from the serious crimes, especially the terrible acts of terrorism, because we must of course recognize: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council. the implementation of all these measures, which i have just briefly mentioned, is part of the wider effort to contribute to the union's objective, enshrined in the treaties, of offering its citizens a common area of freedom, security and justice. this area without any internal borders, where the free movement of people is guaranteed - especially now in this severe pandemic - is the purpose of the schengen area. but the schengen area can only be preserved in a world shaken by crisis and terrorism if we improve security cooperation and put it on a new footing so that freedom and freedom of movement are guaranteed in a lasting way.
vice-president of the commission. – mr president, let me tell you how delighted i am to be able to switch from one pillar of the european way of life, normally public health, to another – security. i welcome this resolution that we’re discussing today. the threats that we face today are many and varied, but they have something in common: they are fast moving, cross—border and cross—sectoral. most of them work across borders, across national borders, across the distinction between online and offline and across the divide of internal and external security. all this points to the very simple fact that we need a joined—up european approach to cope with this evolving landscape of threats, and this is exactly what we did last july when we presented our new eu security union strategy to be coherent and comprehensive, to break down the many barriers and many silos that stand in the way of the eu delivering security to citizens. this strategy is our ambition for the coordinated work for the next five years, and we want it to be understood as a single roof underneath which we can build an edifice with individual rooms, that are now being constructed. these range from combating terrorism and organised crime to preventing and detecting hybrid threats, increasing the resilience of our critical infrastructure, promoting cybersecurity and fostering research and innovation. so i can only therefore welcome the opportunity that you give us through this resolution to be able to discuss our work in these areas. before i go into the design and implementation of the single roof, let me tell you how important we consider the issue and the place of fundamental rights in security policy. this is the touchstone for all our work and is indispensable as our common purpose. i do not see fundamental rights, being in charge of the security union, as an accessory or something that we have to visit ex post. i think that the issue of fundamental rights is a precondition before contemplating any security measures and there are ways to compromise and conciliate these two objectives, which are not as antithetical as some people want to make us believe. let me now come to implementation. implementation of the new security union started immediately on the day of its adoption, with a presentation on the agenda for the fight against drugs and action against firearms, that was also in the previous commission. last week we took another major step forward – the eu agenda on counter—terrorism following the heinous attacks in france and in vienna. we want to strengthen the eu’s work to add value to member states’ responsibilities, to anticipate, prevent, protect from and respond to terrorists. it sets out very concrete actions to help us do precisely that and we have also accompanied this proposal with another one for a reinforced mandate for europol to be able to cope with these new types of threat. europol, as you know, functions as police cooperation. with our proposal for a new mandate they can do much more. let me also welcome the – i would say historic – agreement reached last week on the regulation on terrorist content online. this is something that we need, and it was long overdue. just three hours ago today, the commission announced the strengthening of our cybersecurity strategy, which is most and foremost about security. cybersecurity is not simply a consideration for a market, it’s not a consideration for businesses, it’s a consideration for society and the way we protect our people. the new cybersecurity strategy also includes two legislative proposals for the revision of our tools that need modernisation, like the network and information systems directive (nis), and the critical infrastructure, the physical infrastructure. both these proposals have been presented today. so there our idea is to reinforce and modernise anything that we can do to cope with cyber threats and cyber attacks. let me also highlight the importance of enforcement in all of this. for the first time, in the new nis directive, we have specifically introduced the possibility for fines to facilitate enforcement and make sure that everybody plays by the rules. last week, we also adopted a product which is very popular in this house – the progress report under the security union. this was the first progress report of this commission and it shows that a lot has been done to support member states in the past year. but we need to keep the momentum, and the citizens rightly expect not only pledges but also delivery. there is much discussion and debate on whether this community eu action in the area of security is something that makes the european union compete against our member states. i think the minister put it very right: we do not compete with anyone, we complement and produce added value to allow for the european union to dissipate, organise and respond to these security threats.
on behalf of the ppe group. – mr president, we have a strong set of security rules in place at eu level, but they are only good when they are effectively implemented. these were the words of commissioner schinas last week on the presentation of the progress report, and i think it’s the key message that we also need to put out here today. because he’s right: a lot of work has been done in the past years. we have worked on the eu’s information systems and their interoperability; we have worked on combating terrorism, on protecting borders, on combating child abuse. and yet we see here that the regulations and the directives are in place, but we still have to read – and this is so frustrating – that, for instance, when it comes to the implementation of the anti-terror directive, huge gaps are still there; that there are infringement procedures against 23 member states for not properly following the rules on tackling child abuse. this is, quite frankly, unacceptable, and we need to make sure that the eagerness with which we adopt rules is at least matched by the eagerness with which these rules are implemented, and that is a call on the member states. so i strongly support the commission’s approach on focusing relentlessly on implementation and enforcement. but next to implementation, additional efforts are absolutely needed. european citizens count on and expect us to deliver on security. we need to strengthen europol’s mandate to be better equipped for today’s security challenges. we need to have more and better information-sharing between member states. we need to have a better protection for societies against hybrid threats and foreign interference and disinformation, and we need to find a way to make sure that encryption continues to protect our privacy but no longer provides a shield for criminal behaviour. we must stand together against those who want to destroy our european way of life, and for that you can always count on the support of the epp group.
on behalf of the s&d group. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, on 24 july of this tremendous year 2020, the commission adopted its communication on the eu strategy for a security union, to which the council of interior ministers, meeting on monday 14 december, has contributed to better protect the security rights of european citizens. and i would like to stress that security is a right in itself, a fundamental right enshrined in the charter of fundamental rights of the european union in the same article as the fundamental right to liberty and on an equal footing with it. the commission's communication sets out four strategic priorities: the future, changing threats, terrorism and organised crime - of course! - and a safe ecosystem in which immediate or ongoing initiatives are envisaged: those relating to the prosecution of child sexual abuse, drugs and, of course, the trafficking of firearms. but this european parliament is having its say in this debate. in the committee i chair, the committee on civil liberties, justice and home affairs, we have discussed this commission communication and have made our contribution, noting, of course, the commitment to fundamental rights and the founding values of the union, but also the need to support the fight against terrorism and the implementation and enforcement of existing legislation, because there is anti-terrorism legislation adopted by this european parliament. there is european criminal legislation: there is an anti-terrorism directive in force, from 2012; there is a directive, from 2011, against child sexual abuse and child pornography; and a directive, from 2012, for the protection of victims. but while underlining the importance of implementation and enforcement, it is important that we also bear in mind the need to strengthen our commitment to combating terrorism and organised crime, to combating child sexual abuse, to combating trafficking in human beings along dangerous migratory routes and, of course, to combating drugs. and there the commission has effectively committed itself to a new counter-terrorism agenda, a new anti-drugs agenda, a new cybersecurity agenda. and the committee i chair is committed to finishing the legislative work on the regulation on terrorist content online. but finally i would like to make a reference to the institutional dimension, because there are operational agencies which are subject to the scrutiny of this european parliament: europol, cepol (the agency for police training) and eurojust. i have just participated in the inaugural conference of the platform to combat the encryption used by organised crime: europol joint research centre decryption platform. and it is important that this whole constellation of agencies is used to the full along with prüm to strengthen police cooperation, in a european architecture that has to ensure the interoperability of data in the prosecution of crime and crossborder crime of european scope.
on behalf of the renew group. mr president, mr minister of state, mr vice-president of the commission margarítis schinás, in the fight against crime and terrorism, citizens expect a strong europe. i'd like to focus on europol. we welcome the commission's proposals to strengthen the mandate of europol. the operational coordination of national police services to stop criminals and terrorists abusing borders is strategic. but with my group renew europe we are advocating more ambitious measures to make europol the real arm of the union in the fight against terrorism and organised crime, a true european fbi. we propose to increase europol's powers to collect police data and produce joint analyses between member states, to increase europol's own initiative role in cross-border investigations and to enable the surveillance of foreign fighters on european soil. i would also like to share, mr vice-president, a strong concern about the adequate financing of these measures. the european agencies involved in security - europol, but also frontex, cepol and easo, mentioned by president lópez aguilar - are given new tasks, but they have neither the budgets nor the staffing. let's be more consistent. ladies and gentlemen, we need a strong security policy against terrorism and organised crime to meet the expectations of our fellow europeans.

madam president, i come to this podium with a lot of uncertainty and some certainty. we don't know if it could or couldn't be. we do not know whether, after centuries of fishing in uk waters, our boats will stop fishing there at the inexplicable whim of an irresponsible politician. because yes, that is what brexit was: an irresponsibility, compounded by the inability to reach a reasonable exit agreement so far, mainly on fisheries. and if that is the case, and we do not reach an agreement, the european fleet will have to look for other waters in which to continue fishing. and the british will have to look for other markets, while the whole world will wonder why this nonsense. the position of the european parliament and the council is clear. it has not changed and it must not change at madam president, one year ago, we offered the united kingdom something we had never offered any third country: the most comprehensive partnership that the eu has ever concluded. we stand by our offer. the european parliament has consistently been extremely flexible, patient and constructive throughout the negotiations. we will continue to do everything within our power to avoid a no-deal brexit, because we are aware of the crucial importance of such an agreement for our citizens and our businesses. but we also remain realistic. there are only 13 days left until the end of the transition period. a no-deal has never been closer, and our citizens and businesses deserve clarity and predictability. that is why, today, we are ensuring that planes can keep flying, trucks can keep running and fishermen can keep fishing after 31 december. these contingency measures are of course no substitute for a deal, but they will alleviate, if necessary, the otherwise unavoidable disruption for both eu and uk citizens. because, in spite of michel barnier’s tireless work and dedication, the uk government has still not found the courage to find an agreement. prime minister johnson has yet to acknowledge that every agreement is a compromise and that there is no such thing as having all the benefits of eu membership if you decided to leave. while trade deals are normally about convergence, this agreement deals with future divergence. when sovereign states decide to strike a deal, it is normally not used as a badge to prove their sovereignty. and at a time when all european nations are in the midst of an economic recession due to a pandemic, it would only be sensible to avoid another blow to our businesses and citizens. the european parliament has been patient, but democracy cannot and will not bend to the uk’s lack of responsibility. it is boris johnson who refused to even consider an offer for the transition period. our right to post-democratic scrutiny of any agreement will not be compromised, and there will be no provisional application without parliamentary consent. it is my sincere hope that a deal will be reached – a good and a fair deal for the citizens on both sides of the channel. and it will have been worth it, every single second of it, because as one of the greatest from the uk would say about these negotiations, ‘all’s well that ends well’.
the commission has already made a number of proposals. to understand the debate today about deal or no deal, we also need to look back to 2016, when a majority of the british people said they no longer wanted to be part of this european union. and it would have been the right thing for the european family of nations to have done to have met this decision with respect and to have met the majority of the british people with respect. that was omitted. from the very first moment, a decision which, in the eyes of many, could not be here, which should not have been, was not welcomed with friendship but with disapproval, and punitive sanctions were repeatedly put in place, and the worst thing for me was that the chief negotiator of the european union met the british here with unfriendliness which does not belong to a european family of nations. i am reminded of council president tusk, for example, who would have liked to see the brexiteers go to hell. i am reminded of the parliamentary negotiator verhofstadt who compared the people who pushed for brexit to unlikable people from the french revolution. this is the wrong way, ladies and gentlemen! but the decision was made as it was made. and look at that, the british people on their way out of the european union are suddenly mobile, able to make decisions suddenly. the following are just two examples: the situation in the corona crisis, for example, which affects us all, is that the british have been vaccinating people who want to be vaccinated for two weeks, and the european union has not yet started here. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the animal transport system is not affected by the new rules. ladies and gentlemen, i appeal to you to use the approach of these negotiations to treat the british with friendship and respect. because perhaps one day, when this european union is reformed and the british want to return, they should remember that the goal of the negotiations ended with friendship and respect for them, and not with disapproval and tones that should not be used in political discussion.
madam president, ask not, as the line goes, for whom the bell tolls. as the clock ticks down to 31 december we look forward to ringing in a new year that will surely be an improvement on the one we’ve been through. the bells of the new year will herald hope, but so too will they toll for a bleaker tale, as 31 december marks the end of an era and brexit becomes a harsh reality. this week we are seeing contingency measures being put in place in preparation for a no-deal brexit on issues including transport and fisheries. the measures are a bare, but a crucial, minimum in terms of protecting livelihoods and links with our eu partners. but they do not provide any clarity around protection of our waters or our coastal communities and fishers – the men and the women who bring food to our tables. scouts in ireland say: ‘bí ullamh’ – be prepared. we can but try. ask not for whom the bell tolls, i repeat. brexit will impact on all our lives. and the brexit bell will toll for each and every one of us.
madam president, there’s no doubt about it that break-ups can often be ugly and hostile. this one has certainly been no exception, and i for one am heartily glad that it’s coming to an end, because for four years we’ve been fed a diet in ireland that this is the end of the world as we know it. but you know what? it isn’t, because business is business and a way will be found. it may be the case that some of the new arrangements are disruptive in the short-term, but new arrangements will be found. i just think it’s an awful pity that a lot of this debate was marred by so much sneering and triumphalism. from, on the one hand, claiming aren’t those brits awful eejits that didn’t even know what they were voting for, to being outraged that the royal navy might police british waters. well, why wouldn’t they, when spanish, french and portuguese trawlers are already destroying the seabeds and interfering with our fisheries rights in ireland? so, the idea that the eu was driven by high values while the brits were just out for themselves is utter nonsense. both sides are looking out for themselves. but we should remember here today that 17 million european citizens voted to leave the european union. there are tens of millions more who are being let down. the real division isn’t between the eu and the uk; it’s between the haves and the have-nots. we need to start dealing with that or there’ll be a lot more brexit to come.
madam president, firstly i’m an optimist, so i still hope that there can be a deal and i believe there should be a deal. bear in mind, if we fail to reach an agreement between the european union and the uk, it would have been a failure of politics and diplomacy. but equally, it will have failed livelihoods across the european union - coastal communities in ireland, france and spain, people working in factories in germany and people whose livelihoods will be impacted in the uk as well. so, for all our sakes, i urge everybody involved to ensure that there’s goodwill in these last few hours of negotiations to protect the livelihoods of european citizens. i also want to put on the record my great appreciation to the commission and to michel barnier, who is a household name in ireland at this stage, for the support and effort that he put into ensuring that the northern ireland protocol and the challenging issues and unique circumstances on the island of ireland were very much addressed. and i thank the european union and this parliament for the solidarity with the irish people as well, in the context of brexit negotiations. so, we have just but hours. but these hours are crucial because they could set in train years of dissent between the uk and the european union if we cannot come to an agreement in everybody’s interest. i wish you well and i wish the people of europe a happy christmas and a peaceful new year.
